(b) Branch coverage of the resolver, which should be the strong instrument, reported **45 of 45 `case`s taken and zero gaps** — also false: consecutive `case`s share one body, so istanbul gives a whole fall-through group a single counter. `LIST`, `COL_LIST` and `COL_LIST3` all read exactly 8 because ONE of them was exercised, and identical counts across a group are the signature to look for. Only a by-name measurement across tests AND the tracked corpus distinguishes them.

  **What the new contract pins, beyond presence:** the ten layout aliases resolve through five `case` groups to three components, and the names do not say which axis they produce — `ColList` and `VerticalList` are COLUMN lists while `HorizontalList` is a ROW list; `Column` and `VerticalLayout` are aliases of `Col`. Moving one name into the wrong group during the decomposition would render a flex container either way and change only the axis.

  **Still unmeasured, and the honest limit of this pass:** the vocabulary is one axis of "documented meta capability"; node ATTRIBUTES (`showIf`, `relativeData`, `mapOptions`, `validate`, `normalize`, `relativePath` and the rest) are another, and no equivalent census exists for them. "Named by a test" also remains weaker than "asserted in depth" — it is the floor this gate asks for, not a statement that each capability is thoroughly tested.
2. **Extract pure logic and break the import cycle:** data processing, error mapping, `showIf` evaluation, payload assembly → pure functions in `ui-utils-pack` with direct unit tests (much of `utils.js` already leans this way). ~~This is also where the engine↔form-modules cycle dissolves~~ — **THE CYCLE IS GONE (2026-09-23); the pure-logic extraction is STARTED (2026-09-24), `showIf` first.** Read the two halves separately.

  **`showIf` — done.** It was the first fifty-three lines of `RenderComponent`, a function seven hundred lines long, and there was no way to exercise it except by rendering a tree: its three accepted shapes, the array-row path prefixing and the TWO separate `relativeData: false` escapes were described in comments and asserted nowhere. It is now `engine/showIf.js`, a function of its arguments, with sixteen direct tests. Those tests were mutation-checked rather than trusted for passing — ignoring `equal`, dropping the row prefix, ignoring `relativeData` on a string `showIf` and skipping the live-form merge are each caught. The 38-example DOM snapshot is unchanged, which is what says the extraction moved no behaviour.

  **It lives in the engine, not in `ui-utils-pack` as the item says.** `showIf` is meta semantics rather than a general-purpose helper, and `utils` is below the engine — putting it there would place engine logic under the layer that consumes it, for the same reason `errorsProcessing` stayed put when the registries moved out. Read the item's "pure functions in `ui-utils-pack`" as "pure functions, at the right layer".

  **`onApplyPeriods`'s error handling — done 2026-09-25.** Thirty-five of that action's sixty lines turned a failed API call into something showable, and could not be reached except by making a real call fail with a real `Response`. Now `engine/apiError.js` with twelve direct tests over the body shapes.

  **Two things the extraction measured that nobody could have seen before.** First, jsdom defines no `Response`, so `error instanceof Response` inside the handler throws a ReferenceError the moment an API call fails under test — the message-building code was unreachable in this environment, which is why none of it was covered. A browser has the global and the path works there; the extracted function guards the identifier so it can be tested at all. Second, **a real defect, pinned rather than fixed**: the pass that quotes bare keys runs over the WHOLE body including string values, so a message embedding `errors=[{code:12}]` — the very shape the `message=…errors…` branch exists for — becomes `errors=[{"code":12}]`, whose quotes break the JSON string containing them. Parsing fails and the user is shown the raw body instead of the clean message. Fixing it means parsing first and quoting only as a fallback, which changes what users see and belongs in its own change. **FIXED 2026-09-25, exactly that way:** the body is parsed as it is, and bare keys are quoted only when that fails. Fixing it showed the defect was wider than the pinned case — ANY valid JSON with a `word:` in a string value was hit, a URL's `http:` included, so a message pointing at a status page was shown as raw JSON. Quoting cannot turn valid JSON into different valid JSON, only leave it or break it, so the change is confined to bodies that used to be shown raw. The pinned test was turned round, a URL case added — both fail on the old code — and the fallback's success path, which nothing covered, now has a test that reddens when the fallback is removed.

  **`POPUP_OPEN`'s argument parsing — done 2026-09-25.** The handler's first thirty-seven lines decide WHICH popup was asked for: a meta may write a bare id, an id and options, or one options object carrying the id, and the caller prepends its own arguments first — a click event, sometimes a component class — because `getFunctionFromString` APPENDS a meta's configured arguments to the caller's. That is the same mechanism that makes the state path "the last string argument" in `setStates`, and it is why the filtering exists at all. Now `engine/popupArgs.js` with fifteen direct tests, mutation-checked; the handler is 37 lines shorter and `POPUP_OPEN` is down from 307 lines to 218 across the two extractions.

  **`updateDataOnChange` — done 2026-09-25, and eleven lines held three things.** (1) A STEP-4-CLASS DEFECT THAT STEP 4 MISSED: it ran `replaceDeep(this.data, …)` on the object `this.data` returns, which is the live React state, and only then assigned a clone — so the state React had already handed out was rewritten in place first. `replaceDeepCopy` beside it in `engine/utils.js` returns a copy with the same, unusual semantics (the key is a property NAME and EVERY property of that name anywhere in the tree is replaced — pinned end to end by `rules.dynamic-actions.test.js`). The host's own `data` object was NOT affected, measured: `normalizeIncomingData` copies it on the way in, so this was never visible outside the engine. (2) A LATENT CRASH: a call without a field object — a `Button` naming the action with only a value — died on click with an uncaught `TypeError` from destructuring `undefined`. It is a no-op now, which is what the code already did for a field object without a name; the one call site in the corpus is a toggle, whose `ToggleField` passes `(value, {name})`, so it never hit this. (3) `Array.isArray(params)` removed: a rest parameter is always an array, so the test could not fail.

  **`download` — done 2026-09-25, as `engine/download.js`.** Nearly all command — call the host, read the body, save it, or show a popup — so what moved is the whole handler, with the host call and the failure sink passed in; `config` keeps a three-line binding. The argument handling is the original's exactly: ANY leading object, `null` included, is taken for the click event, and only one is dropped. Direct tests observe the real save through the `download` attribute of the link it clicks; seven mutations of the module and three of the binding each redden a suite. **Two open items it pinned rather than fixed**, both of which change what a user sees: (1) the demo's `button-download_meta.js` passes a second argument, the name to save as, which has been ignored since 2022-11 (`32eb9add`), when this action stopped downloading URLs itself and began calling the host's `downloadFile` — the file is saved under the requested name, path and all. In the demo itself the button does nothing whatever: the example is not flagged `hostApi`, so `Examples.jsx` mounts it without `apiCalls` and the action returns `false`; **(1) FIXED 2026-09-25, and the demo with it:** a second argument that is a non-empty string is the name to save as again, the host still asked for the first. The demo's examples without `hostApi` now get a `downloadFile` that fetches the URL as it is and REJECTS a non-2xx answer; flagging the example `hostApi` would not have done, since the rating service's call treats its argument as a file name there. Verified in the running demo, which also showed why the button had never worked in the dev server: its path chose a prefix by `__DEV__`, which is false in the demo's browser bundle, so it asked for `/ui-render/static/…` and got a 404 — which `fetch` resolves, and the engine then saved as the `.png`. The path is relative now. Whether `__DEV__`/`__PROD__` are also wrong in the LIBRARY build is not measured and is its own task; **and a ninth defect it found, FIXED 2026-09-25:** a failed download CRASHED THE WHOLE UI. The rejection went into the popup's title unchanged, and the popup renders its title as it is — an `Error`, which is what `fetch` rejects with when the network fails, or a `Response`, is an object React cannot render, so the page was replaced by "Objects are not valid as a React child". Measured live for both. `rules.api-actions.test.js` had asserted `title: failure` with the Error object all along, and never saw it because it mocks the popup state. The title is now `describeFailure(err)`: a string as it is, an Error's message, a Response's status. `rules.download-failure.test.js` renders the real popup and fails on the old code; (2) with no file name at all the file is saved as the string `"undefined"`. Asking the host for `undefined` is left alone deliberately: a host may have a default. **(2) FIXED 2026-09-25:** with no name the `download` attribute is set EMPTY, so the browser picks the name — not left out, which would make the click open the blob in the tab instead of downloading it. Chosen by the user over a default name or refusing to download.

  **`upload` — done 2026-09-25, as `engine/upload.js`.** The same shape as `download`: the whole handler moved, with the host call, a reader for the forms' values and what to do with the answer passed in; `config` keeps the `setState` that applies it. Measured first: `delete data[path]` works on a COPY (`compactDataKindArrays` clones), so the step-4 mutation this resembles is not here, and the in-house `Dropzone` does expose `fileInputEl`. Nine mutations of the module and five of the binding were run; the binding's two SURVIVED at first, because the only end-to-end test looked at a `Text`, which reads the data, never an input, which reads the form — a new test now proves `form.restart` puts the answer into inputs. **One line is still uncovered, deliberately left in:** the `key: new Date()` remount (`461541d9`, 2023-03, "rerender instance on file is uploaded"). With `form.restart` covered, the remount's only remaining effect would be resetting component-local state, and neither a test nor a measurement has shown one — which is a reason to measure, not to delete. **Three defects pinned rather than fixed**, each changing what the host receives or what the user sees: (1) the file field is excluded as a TOP-LEVEL key, so a dotted name — the live `upload_meta.js` uses `path.to.file`, though without wiring the action — stays in the payload as `[{}]`, a file list through `JSON.stringify` (**FIXED 2026-09-25:** the field is excluded by path with `unset`; the pinned direct and end-to-end tests were turned round and fail on the old code); (2) only the first of several files is sent, even with `multiple` (**FIXED 2026-09-25, the user's choice of three:** the host gets every picked file as a THIRD argument, `uploadFile(serialized, file, files)`, the first still second — additive, so a host written for two arguments sees no difference, and the public type says so; one call per file and a warning were the alternatives); (3) an answer of `undefined` or `null` replaces the data, and with no data the engine renders an empty container, the upload control included, so the user cannot retry. **FIXED 2026-09-25:** an empty answer — `undefined`, `null`, `''`, `0` — is now ignored and the data kept, the guard `onApplyPeriods` always had. `''` and `0` did not empty the UI but wiped every form value, and fall under the same guard deliberately. The direct and end-to-end tests that pinned the defect were turned round; they fail on the old code, all five. Turning them round also exposed that `toEqual([])` passes for `[undefined]` — Jest ignores undefined array items — so the `undefined` case had never been checked here or in `applyPeriods.test.js`; both now use `toHaveLength(0)`. A host answering a 204 through axios gets `''`, not `undefined`, and is not affected. No live example wires `upload` at all — the one caller left in the corpus is the orphaned `experience_meta.json`.

  **`addData`'s path — done 2026-09-25, as `dataKindPathFor` in `dataKindPush.js`.** Most of this action already lived in `dataKindPush.js` (`rowObjectForDataKindAppend`, `pushDataKindRow`); the one calculation left in `config` was WHERE the `dataKind` map sits in the parent form, and it was written out in full three times — `addData`, `removeData` and `pushDataKindRow`. `addData` and `pushDataKindRow` use the helper now; `removeData` follows in its own change. The handler itself stays in `config`: what is left is instance orchestration (`canSave`, `handleSubmit`, `form.restart`, the touched registry), which moves with `config`. **Measured before it moved:** the whole successful add ran ONCE in the suite, at the root. The branch that resolves a parent row had never run, and neither had the registered-path fallback. `rules.nested-add-data.test.js` now drives the corpus's own `nestedDataKind` example, adding a line to one phase and proving the other phases and the root are untouched. It passes on the code before this change too, as a test of existing behaviour should. The fallback can differ from `''` only if `relativePath` goes from non-empty to empty without re-registration; nothing does that, and it is kept because all three copies had it. **One mutation survives, and why was measured:** computing `addData`'s `existingLen` from the ROOT path instead of the nested one changes nothing observable. The length only picks a row out of PREFIXED draft names, and every draft the suite and the corpus render — the nested example with or without `relativeData: false` included — has unprefixed names, so `rowObjectForDataKindAppend` returns the values whole. Left in, not deleted. **Seen while measuring, not claimed as a defect:** a nested table placed in a parent row's CELLS rather than its `renderItem` does not add at all — both drafts get the same root-level name, `pushDataKindRow` warns that the parent form is unavailable, and a phantom root `dataKind.{kind}: []` appears. No example uses that arrangement.

  **`removeData` — done 2026-09-25, as `removeDataKindRow` in `dataKindPush.js`,** the counterpart of `pushDataKindRow`, and the third copy of the `dataKind` path now goes through `dataKindPathFor` too. The parent INSTANCE and the parent FORM are passed separately because the action reads them at different times — the form, meta and index at click time, the instance when `config` was first read — and that mix is kept exactly. Seven mutations of the helper and two of the binding (a fixed index, no parent form) each redden a suite; its warning branch, never run before, now has direct tests. Three things were measured rather than assumed: (1) the object handed to `reset` is the same one put into state — unlike `pushDataKindRow`, which clones defensively — and with the real final-form that shared reference is HARMLESS: a later edit does not write through it, because final-form updates immutably; pinned, so adding the clone is a decision. (2) The WHOLE suite, 2701 tests, passed with the block's meta dropped from the call. That is the registered-path fallback doing its job, since `registerDataKind` computes it from the same `relativePath`; but it also showed that nothing checked WHICH row a nested remove took, so `rules.nested-remove-data.test.js` now does, on `nestedDataKind`, and dropping the meta AND the fallback reddens it. (3) The action's comment said an index captured when `config` was first read would remove the wrong row once siblings re-index. That could NOT be reproduced: rows are keyed by position, so a captured index stays right, and a mutant capturing it at config time passes every test. Reading it at click time is kept because it is right either way; the claim is no longer repeated as fact.

  **`onApplyPeriods`, the rest — done 2026-09-25, as `engine/applyPeriods.js`.** The error message was lifted into `apiError.js` earlier; the remainder is the same shape as `upload`: every form's values go to the host's `updateExperienceData`, the normalised answer is applied, and a failure is handed on WITH the message `messageFromError` read from it. `config` keeps the `setState` and the popup. Seven mutations of the module and five of the binding each redden a suite — the existing end-to-end test already read the FORM after applying, so `form.restart` was covered here, unlike in `upload`. **One difference from `upload` was put on record here, and resolved since (see `upload` above):** this action ignores an empty answer (`if (!response) return`), and `upload` does not — which is exactly why an empty upload answer empties the UI. The guard here is the obvious fix there, and it belongs in its own change. The unreadable-body case stays unguarded, as `apiError.js` decided: it rejects, and reaches neither the popup nor the console; pinned.

  **`POPUP_OPEN`'s template lookup — done 2026-09-25, as `engine/popupTemplate.js`.** The first of three measured sub-slices of the last and largest handler (227 of the 343 lines left in `config`); the row context and the `PopupContent` class declared INSIDE the handler follow. Coverage before it moved: the second lookup step — the id's base with the instance index put back — never found anything, and the third always did; the exact match on an interpolated id never fired; the fallback at the end of the handler never ran at all; and the `popupById` cache was never reused on creation. The reason for the first two is structural, not a gap: `mapper.js` registers a template ONLY when its id contains `{`, so every key does, and step 2's candidate can match only an id that still carries a placeholder before its number. Step 2 is kept — not provably unreachable — and has a direct test; the binding mutation that drops the instance index therefore survives, as it must. **Two defects pinned, not fixed:** the key becomes a regular expression WITHOUT ESCAPING, so its `.` matches any character (`a.b.{index}` claims `aXb.3`), and a key that is not a valid expression — an unbalanced `[` — throws a SyntaxError; since every key is tried, one such template breaks the pattern lookup for every id on that instance. Escaping is the fix, and a mutation that applies it reddens the pinned tests, so it will be a visible decision. **BOTH FIXED 2026-09-25, in one change because one line fixes both:** the key is split on `{index}` and every other part escaped, so the rest of a key matches literally, and a key with expression characters neither throws nor breaks other ids — it even finds its own. Fixing them apart would have meant a per-key `try/catch` first, which the escaping then makes pointless. The two pinned tests were turned round and fail on the old code. **Terms:** the handler's comment used a domain-specific field name as its example, and `rules.dynamic-actions.test.js` still carried three domain-specific terms in its data; both are renamed to neutral ones. `experienceRating…` is left: it runs through many tests, the demo and the host API's own name (`updateExperienceData`), and renaming it is a sweep of its own.

  **`POPUP_OPEN`'s row context — done 2026-09-25, as `resolvePopupRowContext` in `popupScope.js`,** the second sub-slice, beside the `resolvePopupScope` it continues: the row index, the array path, the row data and the document a template popup is bound to, each with its precedence, and the warning for a row popup with no path. Direct tests cover every precedence step and both row-picking branches — the array fallback had never run in the suite. Ten mutations of the function and two of the binding redden a suite; two binding mutations survive and why is known: dropping the instance's `props` changes nothing, because only a host can set `props.relativePath`, and dropping the instance's `data` changes nothing, because `mapper.js` always registers a template together with its document. **A correction to the source-3 removal above:** it said nothing ever sets `props.relativePath` on an engine instance. Nothing in the ENGINE does — but `library/main.js` spreads the host's props onto the top-level instance, so a host could, though the public types do not declare the prop. That removal was neutral for everything documented and not strictly for that undocumented case. The read in the row context is kept for the same reason.

  **`POPUP_OPEN`'s `PopupContent` — done 2026-09-25, as `createPopupContent` in `engine/components/PopupContent.js`,** the third sub-slice and the last piece of the handler with a boundary. The class was DECLARED INSIDE the handler, and it is still created fresh for every template popup, because that was measured to be load-bearing: with ONE shared class, opening row 1's popup while row 0's is open makes React update the content in place, and row 1's input — correctly named for row 1 — SHOWS row 0's edit, while the form data stays right. That is why nothing in the suite had noticed, and `rules.popup-content.test.js` now pins it: it passes on the old code and reddens on a shared-class mutant. A `key` per popup id would force the same remount with one type, but ids are unique only within an instance, so the fresh type stays. **Where `config` stands after this series:** 228 lines, from about 400; `POPUP_OPEN` 104, from 227; `rules.js` 1059. What is left in the handler is instance orchestration — the `popupById` cache, the empty-items guard, `popupAlert` — and in `config` the bindings, which is the point at which moving `config` to a class member stops being a 400-line shuffle.

  **Found while fixing the demo download, and fixed: every release from 0.32.4 broke a name-only `Image` for every host.** `{view:'Image', name:'logo.png'}` rendered `src="undefined/static/images/logo.png"`, a page-relative URL — a real Chromium network log shows the 404. bb44adda made the library build bake `process.env` to the literal `{NODE_ENV: 'production'}`, so in `dist` __PROD__ is always true and REACT_APP_HOMEPAGE never set, and `common/variables/index.js` wrote the homepage prefix unconditionally. Measured through the supported consumption path — the packed tarball re-bundled by webpack 5 (dev and prod, every define/provide/EnvironmentPlugin variant), esbuild, Vite, a shell variable, a runtime `window.process`, and a Node server render — and in 18 published versions: 0.32.3 was the last good one for ordinary hosts; 0.30.23–0.32.3 read a live `process.env` and so behaved per host (and crashed on a minimal shim). The fix applies the prefix only when a homepage is set, giving `/static/images/logo.png`, the root `static/` payload hosts copy; the maintainer chose to KEEP the baked environment and document it (webpack.library.config.mjs). Tests: an Image assertion in the packed-consumer smoke — the only check of the shipped artifact, it had never rendered an Image — and `common/variables/__tests__/imagesPath.test.js`; both fail on the old code. **Measured alongside, not yet fixed:** in the demo builds (dev, GitHub Pages, the Playwright e2e build) dotenv-webpack stubs `process.env`, so every flag is false — __DEV__ is false in development and __PROD__ in production. Nothing visible depends on it today. Its fix (read `process.env.X` as literals and derive the demo homepage from `publicPath`) changes nothing for library hosts and is the next change, never in its NODE_ENV-only form, which was measured to put "undefined/static/images/" into the demo. **FIXED in the following change, as chosen:** `_envs.ts` reads `process.env.NODE_ENV` and `process.env.REACT_APP_HOMEPAGE` as literals (exported `HOMEPAGE`), `webpack.demo.config.mjs` defines the homepage from `publicPath` ahead of Dotenv, and `common/variables` and `routes.js` use it. Measured: the demo now reports __DEV__ in development and __PROD__ on GitHub Pages, with images under /ui-render/static/images/ where CopyPlugin emits them; the e2e build keeps /static/images/; the library is unchanged. Guarded by `npm run test:env-flags` (in CI) and `_envs.bundled.test.js`, both failing on the code before it. One thing a build shell can still do: an exported NODE_ENV beats `--mode` (dotenv-webpack's system variables register first) and webpack only warns — true before this change for React's own build, and the harness scrubs it. The flag icons in `renders.js` are tree-shaken out of `dist`, so `flags/` is not requested by any host today.

  **Still to extract:** the data processing in `rules.js`. Payload assembly turns out to be DONE already — `getFormsData`, `getRawFormsData`, `getLiveMergedDataKindArray` and `compactDataKindArrays` live in `engine/utils.js` and `dataKindPush.js`, and the methods on the instance are thin wrappers over them; this item read as outstanding only because nobody had checked.

  **How the cycle went.** `src/core/state/` is the new neutral layer, and it holds ONLY the three registries both sides write to — `formsStorage`, `errorsMap`, `storedTouched` — which used to live one on each side of the boundary. They are re-exported from where they were declared, so no call site moved. The fourth name the item lists, `errorsProcessing`, deliberately did NOT move: it reads a meta node and writes the error map, which is engine business, and putting it in a neutral module would have placed engine logic BELOW the layer that uses it. It is handed to `withForm` as a callback instead, through the options bag that already existed. The two `eslint-disable no-restricted-imports` comments that marked the exception are deleted, and `src/core/state/**` has its own layer rule — verified by adding an illegal import and watching it fail, because a layer rule that never fires is the §9.9-H5 mistake repeated.

  **What the cycle was hiding, and it is a real defect, not a test artifact.** `engine/rules.js` registered six `FIELD.TYPE` names — `AutoSubmit`, `Data`, `Icon`, `Image`, `Popup`, `TableCells` — while `engine/mapper.js` dispatches on all six and `rules.js` imports `mapper.js`. The resolver depended on a constant its own importer installed, and the cycle guaranteed the importer always loaded. With the cycle gone, anything loading the mapper without `rules.js` got a `switch` comparing against `undefined` for those six, making six documented views silently unreachable. Two mapper tests failed within a minute of the cycle being cut. The constants now sit with the other 30 in `modules/variables/fields.js`; the generated view reference is byte-identical, and the generator's declaring-sites tripwire was updated to match.
3. **De-globalize instance state** — ~~**STARTED 2026-09-23: `Active.translate` is done, the rest is not.**~~ **DONE (re-checked 2026-10-01): every item below is closed or was found not to leak, and R14 is retired on the same evidence (§11).** The engine writes instance-bound state into module globals — ~~`Active.translate` (`rules.js:245`)~~ **(CLOSED: each instance now builds its own translator in its constructor and the mapper reads THAT, falling back to the global only for nodes rendered without an instance; an embedded instance inherits its owner's)**, ~~the module-level `errorHandlerFunction`~~ **(CLOSED 2026-09-23 — the module-level `let` is deleted, not shadowed: nothing else read it. Measured before the fix, with two documents on one page: touching the FIRST one's required field delivered its error to the SECOND one's callback, and the first one's own callback was never called at all. THE OTHER HALF OF THIS CHANNEL IS ALSO CLOSED 2026-09-24: `errorsMap` was shared, so once the other document re-rendered for any reason its `componentDidUpdate` found these errors in that map and reported them as its own. It is now keyed by the final-form `form` object — the key BOTH sides already hold, so no signature changed and neither side needed a reference to the other: `errorsProcessing(form, meta)` takes it as an argument, the engine reads `this.form`, and the form module's subscription closes over the same object. A `WeakMap`, so an unmounted form's errors are collected with it and fourteen test harnesses stopped needing a global reset between cases. The pinned assertion in `rules.two-instances-errors.test.js` flipped from recording the wrong behaviour to recording the right one, which is what pinning a defect is for)**, and ~12 action handlers bound to `this` on the shared `FIELD.FUNC` registry (`rules.js:616–1081`, resolved at render time through `transforms.js`) — **MEASURED 2026-09-24 and found NOT to leak, so nothing to convert.** Two documents, each with a `Button` whose `onClick` is `submit` and its own `onSubmit`: clicking each one reaches its own handler, before and after the other mounts. The engine rewrites the shared registry on every render and immediately snapshots it into `funcConfig` (`fieldFunc: {...FIELD.FUNC}`), so the per-instance binding the code comment claims is real. This clause stays in the inventory as a CHECKED item rather than a debt; if a future change stops taking that snapshot per render, it becomes one again. Today two `UIRender` instances on one host page silently hijack each other's actions/translate, and StrictMode's double-invocation exercises exactly these writes — a decomposition that skipped this step would still fail the workstream's own acceptance gate. Inventory all module-global mutable state — `Active.*`, `FIELD.FUNC`, `errorHandlerFunction`, translation registries **(CHECKED 2026-10-01, nothing to convert: every `localiseTranslation` call in `src/core` runs when its module loads, and the language the phrases are read in, `Active.LANG`, is assigned nowhere outside the tests. The same check found no write to `Active` from an instance but the `translate` fallback above: `renderField`, `Field` and `UIRender` are code, the same for every document, and `passwordCheck` is the host's to set)**, plus `formsStorage`, `errorsMap`, `formInitialValues`, `storedTouched` (§2.6-5) **MEASURED, then CONVERTED TOGETHER WITH `formInitialValues` 2026-09-24.** Both are per form now, keyed by the final-form object like the error map. `formsStorage` was left shared, as the way one form reads the others' data; **that read crossed documents, and since 2026-10-06 it stays within one document tree** (§11 R14). The measurement that led there is kept because it is what decided the shape: `storedTouched` is shared, but four scenarios failed to produce any cross-document symptom: two documents with the same field name at mount; the second re-rendering afterwards; a fresh document opened after another was touched; and a second document appearing beside a touched one. The reason is that the display condition is `storedTouched[name] || touched || !pristine`, so final-form's OWN `touched` dominates it whenever the field is still mounted — the registry only matters for the case it was built for, a field that re-rendered and lost that flag. What the probes DID show is a real coupling with no symptom today: mounting a second document EMPTIES `storedTouched` for everybody, because the subscription clears it whenever `formInitialValues` — itself a module global, and one this inventory does not list — differs from the incoming `initialValues`. So the wipe and the sharing are the same mechanism, and converting one without the other would leave the clearing keyed on a global that still belongs to whichever form initialised last. They were done as one slice. A field reaches its form through the `instance` the engine gives every field (`mapper.js:640` passes it on every `renderField` call), and a field built without one — which happens only in tests — has no remembered touches to consult. The contract that pins the fix is `utils.form-lifecycle.test.js`, "does not let a second form reset the first one when it initialises"; it fails on the previous code. **And it surfaced a third instance of the same defect class as §9.3 step 2's:** `engine/utils.js` compares a meta node against `FIELD.TYPE.SELECT`, which `modules/form/constants.js` registered — so Select reordering worked only because the engine happened to import the form module for `storedTouched`. Removing that import broke six tests in a suite that never mentions the form module. Those five field views now sit in `variables/fields.js` with the rest of the vocabulary, and the generated reference is unchanged. — and move it to per-instance context; ~~make the modal portal root per-instance too~~ **CLOSED 2026-09-24, and the item understated it.** Every mounted document rendered the same fixed `id="render-popup-root"` and `Popup` found it with a document-wide `getElementById`, so the FIRST one in the DOM received every popup. Two consequences the item does not name and a measurement found: the shell carries the `lang--*` and currency classes, so a second document's popup rendered under the FIRST document's language and currency; and unmounting the first document would take the second one's open popup with it. `AppWrapper` now publishes the element itself on the existing `AppContext` — a NODE, not an id — and `Popup` portals into that, falling back to the id only when nothing published one, which is how the demo and the harnesses supply a root. The id stays on the element: it is part of the shell a host sees, and nothing depends on it for finding the root any more. Pinned by `library/__tests__/popup-root.two-instances.test.js`, which fails on the previous code. Add a two-instances-on-one-page case to the contract suite (R14). **R14 EXISTS NOW** (`engine/__tests__/rules.two-instances.test.js`), and building it corrected how this item should be read: **mounting two instances does not expose the defect at all.** React constructs and renders each sibling in turn, so every instance reads the global it has just written and the output looks right — a mount-only test would have reported the hijack as fixed before anything was. It appears on the first RE-RENDER: by then the sibling owns the global, and the re-rendering instance uses the sibling's value permanently, since it never writes the global again. Measured on the previous code: mount gave `A:hello` + `B:hello`, and the re-render turned the first into `B:hello`. Any further global this step de-globalizes needs a test of that shape, not a mount.
4. **Fix the catalogued runtime hazards (§2.6-6):** ~~deduplicate the per-render `form.subscribe()` and keep/call its unsubscribe~~ **— CLOSED, re-measured 2026-09-17** (the same fact §2.6-6a already records as done, which this list contradicted): `modules/form/utils.js:380-386` subscribes once per active form and tears the previous subscription down when the form is replaced, and `componentWillUnmount` at `:421-426` calls the stored unsubscribe and nulls both `_unsubscribeForm` and `_subscribedForm`; the file contains no other `subscribe` call. **Two of the five are now CLOSED (2026-09-23), both debounce-lifetime bugs and both fixed together because they shared a prerequisite that the item did not anticipate: `debounce` had no way to cancel.** It closed over its timer and returned a bare function, so "cancel AutoSave's debounce on unmount" was not implementable as written. `debounce` gained a `.cancel()` first — additive, and the only change to a util used at five call sites.

  - ~~make the debounced `handleChangeInput` per-instance instead of prototype-shared~~ ✅ `Class.prototype.handleChangeInput = debounce(…)` called `debounce` ONCE, so every instance of a decorated class shared a single timer. **Demonstrated before fixing:** two instances, both typing, and the first one's `syncInputChanges` never ran — cancelled by the second, whose call then executed against the LAST `this`. Invisible with one form on the page, which is why it survived; the demo never renders two. Now a prototype accessor builds the debounced function on first access and caches it per instance, and `componentWillUnmount` cancels it.
  - ~~cancel `AutoSave`'s debounce on unmount~~ ✅ **and a second leak in the same component that the item did not name:** `UNSAFE_componentWillReceiveProps` REPLACED `this.handleChange` when `delay` changed without cancelling the old one, so a save scheduled under the previous delay still fired. Both pinned by tests that fail on the old code.

  - ~~move the setState-in-render Dropdown branch into an effect/derivation~~ ✅ **CLOSED 2026-09-23, and NOT as this item proposed.** Neither an effect nor a derivation was needed: the `selectPreviousValue` it maintained is write-only — its only reader is its own next write — so it moved off React state onto a plain instance field, which is the smallest change that removes the update while keeping the one behaviour the branch has (a one-shot `props.value` override on the transition to an empty value). Measured before the fix: React warned "Cannot update during an existing state transition" and every Dropdown field rendered TWICE. Note what this does and does not buy: it removes the state update, not the write, so the render is not pure — full purity is step 6's business. `asInputDateField` declared the same state key and never used it; deleted.
  - ~~stop mutating state via `set(this.state, …)` (clone or immutable update)~~ ✅ **CLOSED 2026-09-23.** A new `setIn()` — the immutable counterpart of `set()`, written beside it in `lodash-lite.ts` so the two cannot drift on the empty path, the keys they refuse or the container a missing segment creates — copies only along the path, so untouched branches keep their identity. "Clone", the item's other suggestion, would have been wrong: a `cloneDeep` of state on every update breaks the identity of every branch and costs a re-render everywhere. **The half the item did not see, and the one that matters:** each site passes an UPDATER to `setState`. A copy built from `this.state` is built from the state as it was before every other update in the same batch, so two writes into one container undo each other — something the MUTATING version could not do, because the second call saw the first one's result. The whole suite passed with that regression in place until a test batched `setState` the way React does.
  - ~~stop re-creating the `autoSubmit` debounce every render~~ ✅ **CLOSED 2026-09-23.** `engine/autoSubmit.js` keeps one debounced submit per instance AND delay, cancelled at unmount. Per delay rather than one per instance: a meta may declare `autoSubmit` on several nodes with different delays, and a single slot would have them evicting each other on every render pass — the original defect, made worse. Measured before the fix: two changes with a render between them submitted twice.

  **This step is now empty.** All five of its fixes shipped before the decomposition, exactly as the item predicted they could. Risk R17 retires with it.
5. **Replace mutation with composition:** convert prototype patching into explicit HOC/wrapper composition (`withUIRenderLifecycle(Component)`) — same behavior, but visible in the component tree and StrictMode-analyzable. **DONE 2026-09-28 (started 2026-09-24).** Neither `rules.js` nor `form/utils.js` mutates a class it is handed, and every member of the engine layer and the form layer is written in its class body. The only prototype assignments left are the two deliberate `state` shapes (the end of this item). What remains of the workstream is steps 6 and 7.

  **What the measurement changed about the shape.** The item proposes an HOC, and for the engine that is the wrong instrument: `Decorator`, `withDataKind` and `withFormSetup` have exactly ONE consumer each — `UIRender` — so there is no reuse for an HOC to provide, and this codebase has already been bitten twice by the wrapper-versus-instance confusion an extra layer creates (§9.3 step 2, step 3). Only `@withTimer` is a genuine shared HOC, on seven components, and it already cleans up correctly. So the layer became a SUBCLASS: `Decorator` declares `class UIRenderLifecycle extends Class {}` and installs onto that, leaving the class it is handed exactly as written.

  **The load-bearing detail, found before the edit and not in the item.** `engine/Data.js` renders nested documents through `Active.UIRender`, which pointed at the RAW class — it worked only because the prototype was mutated. `Active.UIRender` is now the layer, which is also what it always meant: the engine class without the form wrapper around it. Three test suites reached for `UIRender.prototype` for the same reason and now read the layer through the same channel.

  **Also measured: three of the five "call the original" guards were dead.** Instrumenting `Decorator` at decoration time showed `componentWillUnmount`, `UNSAFE_componentWillMount` and `UNSAFE_componentWillUpdate` were all `undefined` on the class being decorated; only `UNSAFE_componentWillReceiveProps` and `componentDidUpdate` have a real original. With a subclass those captures read genuine parent methods, which is what "the original" always meant.

  **The members moved into member position 2026-09-25 — all but `config` and `state`.** Four getters, a setter and seven methods are written as class members now, and the four captured originals are gone: `if (componentWillUnmount) componentWillUnmount.apply(this, arguments)` reads `if (super.componentWillUnmount) super.componentWillUnmount(...arguments)`, which is what those captures always meant. Both surviving parent calls are covered — deleting either one reddens the suite.

  **`state` stays a prototype assignment, and that is load-bearing rather than tidy.** `withFormSetup` MERGES onto it — `Class.prototype.state = {…, ...Class.prototype.state}`, and since 2026-09-28 `FormSetup.prototype.state = {…, ...Class.prototype.state}` on a class of its own, which reads the engine's the same way — so a class FIELD, which initialises per instance AFTER `super()` returns, would leave that merge reading `undefined` and silently drop the engine's state shape. Two test harnesses read `prototype.state` directly for the same reason. Measured before the edit, not discovered by it.

  **`config` moved into member position 2026-09-28, ~~which finishes the class syntax in `rules.js`~~.** **(Corrected the same day: it does not. `withDataKind` still assigns four methods — `getDataKindPath`, `registerDataKind`, `unregisterDataKind`, `getDataKind` — onto the layer's prototype after the class, and this entry missed them.)** It was the four-hundred-line getter that built the action handlers, and it waited until §9.3 step 2 had lifted most of them into modules of their own, so what moved was 225 lines, and they moved byte for byte: apart from comments, the only lines that differ are the three wrapper lines and the new `get config () {`. The diff looks larger because git reports the rest of the class moving past the body instead, which `git diff --color-moved` makes visible. The property descriptor changed in one respect, `configurable` false → true, and nothing depends on it: `get meta` is the getter's only reader, nothing in `src` enumerates prototype members, and `form/utils.js`, which does define members on this prototype, never touches `config`. The layer is pinned by `rules.lifecycle-layer.test.js`, which fails on the code before the subclass.

  ~~**Still open in this step:** `withFormSetup` in `form/utils.js`, which still writes onto the layer's prototype when it is decorated. It merges `state` (the one prototype assignment `rules.js` keeps for this reason), merges `propTypes`, defines ten members with `defineProperty`, assigns `syncInputChanges`, and REPLACES the layer's own `UNSAFE_componentWillReceiveProps` and `componentWillUnmount` with wrappers that call the captured originals: the capture-and-patch pattern this step removed from `rules.js`, still applied to the class `rules.js` now declares.~~ **CLOSED 2026-09-28, below.**

  **`withFormSetup` stopped patching 2026-09-28.** It now returns `class FormSetup extends Class`, named `<Class>WithFormSetup` the way `asField` names its classes, and leaves `Class` as written. `withForm` renders what it returns and publishes it as the wrapper's `WrappedComponent`; `rules.js` points `Active.UIRender` at that, so nested documents still render exactly the class the wrapper renders. The chain is now the declared class, the engine layer, then the form layer. The three captured originals are gone: the two lifecycle wrappers and the debounced `handleChangeInput` reach the class below through `super`. `state` stays a prototype assignment on the form layer too, and there it is load-bearing twice over: a field would initialise per instance after `UIRender`'s constructor had built the state, and overwrite it.

  **Measured before and after, not assumed.** A probe dumped everything the form layer makes observable on the real chain.
  - **Unchanged:** the set of members an instance reaches; every getter body; the prop types (keys, order and validators); the prototype `state`; and the fact that the wrapper renders the class nested documents render.
  - **Changed, with no reader:** nine getters are now `configurable` (class accessors are), and `syncInputChanges` is no longer enumerable. Nothing in `src` spies on, redefines or enumerates any of them.
  - **Changed, visible:** the rendered class's name, which appears in the two required-prop warnings a nested document emits. It goes from `UIRenderLifecycle` to `UIRenderLifecycleWithFormSetup`, and both allowlists still match it.

  Five test harnesses built instances from the class they had passed in, relying on the mutation; they now use what `withFormSetup` returns. `utils.form-layer.test.js` and `rules.lifecycle-layer.test.js` pin the new shape with nine tests that fail on the previous code. Each piece of the new wiring is covered: removing either `super` lifecycle call, the inherited-handler call, the prototype `state`, the prop types, the `Active.UIRender` publication or the rendered class makes the suite fail, with between 1 and 298 failing tests per removal.

  ~~**Still open in this step:** `withDataKind` in `rules.js`, the last mixin that patches a prototype. It assigns its four registry methods onto the engine layer's prototype after the class. That is the layer `rules.js` itself declares, not a class it is handed, so nothing escapes; converting it is the same change as this one, on a smaller scale.~~ **CLOSED 2026-09-28, below, and not quite as predicted.**

  **`withDataKind` folded into the engine layer 2026-09-28, which closes this step.** Its four methods (`getDataKindPath`, `registerDataKind`, `unregisterDataKind`, `getDataKind`) are members of `UIRenderLifecycle` now. The mixin is gone rather than turned into a subclass factory the way `withFormSetup` was, which is where the prediction above was wrong. `withFormSetup` is the form module's own API, which `withForm` applies to whatever class it is handed. `withDataKind` had exactly one consumer, the class `rules.js` itself declares, so a layer of its own would have added a link to the chain for no reuse. That is the reasoning that made the engine layer a subclass rather than an HOC.

  The two suites that applied the mixin to bare classes now run the same assertions on the engine layer's prototype; `withDataKind.test.js` became `rules.data-kind-registry.test.js`. The same probe as for `withFormSetup` found the chain, every level's own members and every body unchanged. The one difference is that the four methods are no longer enumerable, so a `for…in` over an instance no longer lists them, only `setState`, `forceUpdate`, `isReactComponent` and `state`. Nothing in `src` enumerates an instance or a prototype. `rules.lifecycle-layer.test.js` now pins that every member of both layers is written in its class body, `state` excepted; it fails on the previous code and names the four.

  **What is left of the pattern in `src`** is outside the engine. `@withTimer` (`components/utils/hocs.js`), on seven components, still assigns three methods onto the class it decorates and replaces its `componentWillUnmount` with a wrapper that calls a captured copy. It is the genuine shared HOC the shape note above names, and it cleans up correctly. It went in step 6, with the last class it decorated.
6. **Hooks form (final state):** lifecycle logic as hooks (`useUIRenderData`, `useFormIntegration`), classes retired. **STARTED 2026-09-28, leaf components first. All nine leaves converted by 2026-09-29.** The engine's six definitions are what is left: 12 `UNSAFE_*` lines, their `super` calls included, in `rules.js` and `form/utils.js`, measured after the last leaf. **The engine's design pass: 2026-09-29, at the end of this step.** **The engine: DONE 2026-09-29, in the seven slices at the end of this step.** No `UNSAFE_*` lifecycle is left in `src`. The document's classes were kept rather than retired: they are the classes of the instance that a function component hosts (slice 6).

  **Measured before starting.** There are 21 `UNSAFE_*` lines in 11 files. §7's table said 26 in 12 on 2026-09-17; step 5 moved the engine's into class members but did not remove them.
  - **Engine:** six real definitions: `UIRender`'s `componentWillReceiveProps`; the engine layer's `componentWillMount`, `componentWillUpdate` and `componentWillReceiveProps`; and `componentWillReceiveProps` in both `WithForm` and the form layer. The remaining engine lines are their `super` calls.
  - **Leaves:** one each in nine components: `ProgressBar`, `ProgressSteps`, `Counter`, `Expand`, `StandaloneTabs`, the engine's `Tabs`, `TableView`, `InputNative` and `AutoSave`. Six of the nine are `@withTimer` classes.

  §2.3 calls every leaf "a props→state derivation, mechanically convertible". Two come close: `ProgressBar` and `ProgressSteps` mirror a value into state and cancel a pending timer. The others also do one of these:
  - drive transition timers (both `Tabs`, `Expand`);
  - restart an animation (`Counter`);
  - resize a DOM node before paint (`InputNative`);
  - swap a debounce (`AutoSave`);
  - keep render caches (`TableView`, both `Tabs`, `Expand`).

  **The order.** The leaves go one at a time, smallest first, each becoming a function component. When the last `@withTimer` class goes, `withTimer` goes with it, and with it the last prototype-patching decorator in `src`. The engine comes last, after a design pass of its own: the mapper, every field, nested documents and popup templates all read its `instance`, so the hooks form has to keep that contract. One class stays whatever happens: `Render.js` is the error boundary, and React has no hook for `componentDidCatch`.

  **`ProgressBar` converted 2026-09-28, the first leaf.** It was a `@withTimer` `PureComponent` and is now `React.memo` of a function.
  - **A changed `value`** is applied during render. That is React's documented replacement for deriving state from props, and the same point at which the lifecycle applied it.
  - **The mount fill-in** is an effect. Its cleanup cancels the fill-in when the value changes or the component unmounts, which is what `clearTimer()` did.
  - **The comparison is `Object.is`.** `NaN` is documented input, and with `!==` the render-time derivation never settles; React stops it with "Too many re-renders".

  Nine behaviour tests read `@withTimer`'s `timers` array through a ref. They now spy on `setTimeout` and `clearTimeout` and read only the timers scheduled with the component's own delay, and they pass unchanged against the previous class. Six mutations of the new component each fail at least one of them: the cleanup, the render-time derivation, `Object.is`, the effect's dependencies, the mount-value guard, and the empty first render.

  **`ProgressSteps` converted 2026-09-28, with `useTimers`.** `useTimers` (`components/utils/timers.js`) is what replaces `@withTimer` for the four leaves still on it: the timers a function component starts outside an effect, from a click handler for instance, all cleared at unmount.

  ProgressSteps' lifecycle re-derived the active step on every parent render, and in controlled mode it cleared a pending click. The trigger of `UNSAFE_componentWillReceiveProps` is a new props object, not a changed value, and the conversion keeps that:
  - The render-time derivation compares the props object, which a re-render for the component's own state leaves unchanged.
  - A pending click is superseded through a counter that the timer checks when it fires, rather than cleared from render.
  - The active step and the transition are one state object, merged like `setState`, so the unbatched timer updates of React 16 and 17 commit as often as before.
  - It is not memoised. `React.memo` would skip a parent render with equal props, which the lifecycle did not. Under UI Render the mapper rebuilds `items` on every render, so the class never bailed out there anyway.

  Three new tests pin behaviour the old suite left open: ~~a controlled parent render with an unchanged `activeIndex` still supersedes a click~~ (flipped 2026-10-05, below), an uncontrolled one does not, and a second click replaces the first. All three pass against the previous class. Six mutations of the component and three of the hook each fail at least one test.

  **A candidate defect, pinned rather than fixed.** In controlled mode, any parent render within the 50 ms transition swallows the click, because the class cancelled it on every parent render, not only when `activeIndex` changed. The engine's own `Tabs` stopped doing the equivalent for exactly that reason (its note on `itemsChanged`). Under UI Render the parent re-renders often; whether that swallows real clicks has not been measured. **MEASURED AND FIXED 2026-10-05.** In Chromium, through the `/demo` sandbox, a `ProgressSteps` bound to state (`activeIndex: {name: '{state.step,0}'}`, `onChange: setState`) took a click and, at once, new `data` from the host: master landed 0 of 5 such clicks, and the fix 5 of 5. A controlled `activeIndex` now supersedes a pending click only when it differs from the active step, as the engine's `Tabs` does. The pinning test is flipped, and an engine-level test drives the case through the published entry; both fail on the previous code. A parent that does not take the click still brings the step back on its next render, and a test pins that too.

  **`Counter` converted 2026-09-28**, the third leaf and the second on `useTimers`. Its lifecycle compared the values of six animation props, not the props object as ProgressSteps' did. So it is memoised, like the `PureComponent` it was: a parent render with equal props had nothing to restart.
  - A changed animation prop resets the displayed value during render.
  - The delay and the frames run in one effect per animation, and its cleanup cancels them all.
  - The default `easingFn` is a module constant. An inline default would be a new function on every render and would restart the animation every time; a mutation doing exactly that fails with React's "Too many re-renders".
  - A `NaN` prop compares equal to itself. The class's `!==` restarted a `NaN` animation on every parent render, and compared during render that would never settle. This is the one behaviour that changed.

  The frame step is now a pure, exported `nextFrame`, so the test that called the class's `animate` through a ref calls it instead. The three tests that read `@withTimer`'s `timers` array use the same spies as ProgressBar's and pass against the previous class. Six mutations each fail at least one test.

  **A second trap, found by `test:coverage` and not by any test: `defaultProps` turned into default parameters become branches.** `Counter.js` has a 100% branch threshold. No test rendered a counter without `start`, which cost nothing while the default was `defaultProps` data, and failed the threshold once it was a default parameter. The remedy is a test for the documented default, not a lower threshold. Expand and StandaloneTabs carry per-file thresholds too.

  **`AutoSave` converted 2026-09-28**, the fourth leaf and the first not on `@withTimer`. Its lifecycle rebuilt the debounce when `delay` changed, cancelling the old one first (§9.3 step 4). The debounce is now memoised per `delay`, and an effect cancels the one it replaces, and the last one at unmount.
  - **That cancel happens one commit later than the class's.** The class cancelled in the lifecycle, before the render, so a save coming due between the render and the effect would now still run. No test can see that moment, because the tests flush effects synchronously. A check that would have closed it was left out for that reason: it would have been an untested branch.
  - **The baseline values and the save in flight are refs,** since nothing renders them. The class kept the values in state and the promise on the instance.

  Two new tests pin what the old suite left open, and both pass against the previous class: a save goes through the latest render's `onChange` and `partial`, and a partial save is diffed against the values last saved. Seven mutations each fail at least one test. An eighth, dropping the `return` after the baseline is first recorded, is equivalent: the baseline diffed against itself is empty.

  **Checked under `<StrictMode>` for step 7:** the simulated remount does not lose the first save, in the class or in the function. FormSpy's second subscription delivers the form state again, so the baseline is recorded after the remount.

  **`StandaloneTabs` converted 2026-09-28**, the fifth leaf and the third on `useTimers`. It reaches only the demo.
  - **Parent renders.** Its lifecycle ran on every parent render, like ProgressSteps', so it detects a parent render by the new props object and is not memoised.
  - **Controlled `activeIndex`.** The lifecycle also did two things beyond setting state: it started the 50 ms transition timer for a controlled `activeIndex`, or reported an immediate change. Those now happen in an effect, a commit later. The state itself is still set during render.
  - **The handle.** Function content and children still receive `props`, `state`, `tabs`, `contents` and `setTab`, in one object for the component's lifetime, as `this` was.

  **One behaviour changed, measured first and pinned.** The lifecycle compared a controlled `activeIndex` with the committed active tab. The demo's `NavTabs` follows a click by navigating, which passes that tab back as its `activeIndex` in the same batch. The class then started a second transition and reported the click twice: `[2, 2]` under test on React 16, 17 and 18. In the demo that meant two history entries per tab click, measured with a `pushState` counter. Compared with the current state, the click is reported once. A new test pins that, and it fails on the previous class. Five more new tests pin behaviour the old suite left open, and all five pass against the previous class:
  - a controlled change with no `transitionUpdate` still waits for the transition;
  - a finished transition reports through the latest `onChange`;
  - the handle is the same object on every render;
  - a parent render brings back a tab the parent did not adopt;
  - function children switch tabs at once through the handle's `setTab(index, false)`. The lifecycle used to reach that path, which covered it for free. Now only a caller of the handle does, and `test:coverage` caught the uncovered line.

  Eight mutations each fail at least one test.

  **Found while measuring, and fixed in its own change (#111).** The demo's `NavTabs` navigated on every report, including the report of a change that came from the URL itself. So a Back that changed the tab pushed a new entry and discarded the Forward history: once with the function, twice with the class. With the class, the first Back only landed on the duplicate entry, so the tab did not change. `NavTabs` now navigates only when the path differs, and `NavTabs.history.test.js` counts the pushes.

  **`Expand` converted 2026-09-28**, the sixth leaf and the fifth of the six `@withTimer` classes. Only the engine's `Tabs` is still on the decorator.
  - **Memoised,** like the `PureComponent` it was. Its lifecycle compared two values, `children` and `expanded`, so a parent render with equal props had nothing to do.
  - **A changed `expanded`** moves the state during render. `undefined` toggles, as the default argument of the class's `update()` did. `undefined` and `false` stay different values, so a first `expanded={false}` is a collapse.
  - **The report to `onClick`, and the timer that ends a collapse,** run in an effect keyed on the change, a commit later. The class ran them in a `setState` callback. The effect's cleanup cancels the timer when the next change comes or the component unmounts. It is the component's only timer and belongs to that effect, so, like ProgressBar, it needs no `useTimers`.
  - **Expanding is one commit, not two.** The class first committed the content collapsed and then expanded it from the `setState` callback, in the same task, so the browser never painted the first commit. Measured in the demo: both of the class's commits reach one `MutationObserver` callback, before the next animation frame, with the same five DOM mutations as the function's single commit.
  - **Lazy children** are called once per `children`, on the first render that shows them, as the class's `content` getter cached them. One exception, measured on all three legs: under `<StrictMode>` in development, an `Expand` that mounts expanded calls them twice. React renders a function component's mount twice with fresh hooks, while the class's second render reused the instance and its cache. That matters only to a `children` function with side effects, which is what StrictMode is there to expose.

  **Two behaviours changed, both measured first and pinned.**
  - **A parent that follows the report.** The lifecycle compared a changed `expanded` with the committed state. So when a parent followed a report by passing the new state back as `expanded`, the class expanded a second time and reported again: `[true, true]` under test on React 16, 17 and 18, the real TableView's `handleItemExpand` included. The demo's Examples page is such a parent. In the running demo, every example opened pushed its URL onto the history twice: four entries for two examples, against two now. Compared with the current state, an expansion is reported once.
  - **The next change cancels a pending collapse.** The class kept every collapse's timer, so collapsing, expanding and collapsing again within `duration` unmounted the content when the first timer fired. In the demo that was about 200 ms into the second collapse's 500 ms animation. The content now stays until the second animation is over.

  The 14 suites that render an `Expand`, 203 tests, pass unchanged on all three legs. Two new tests pin the two changes and fail against the previous class. Six more pin behaviour the old suite left open, and all six pass against the previous class:
  - a controlled `expanded` going back to `undefined` toggles;
  - a first `expanded={false}` is a collapse;
  - lazy children are called once across collapsing and expanding again;
  - each change is reported once under `<StrictMode>`;
  - only a collapse starts a timer, and unmounting cancels it;
  - a parent render with equal props does not render it again.

  Twelve mutations each fail at least one test. The last test exists because of one of them: dropping `React.memo` failed nothing until it was written.

  **The engine's `Tabs` converted 2026-09-29**, the seventh leaf and the last `@withTimer` class. `withTimer` is deleted with it, along with `hocs.js`, the one file it lived in, and its contract test, so no decorator is left in `src`. It is converted the way StandaloneTabs was, the other of the two copies (§9.9-H6):
  - **Parent renders.** Its lifecycle ran on every parent render and compared a controlled `activeIndex` with the state, so it detects a parent render by the new props object and is not memoised. A test pins that a parent render with equal props still brings back a tab the parent did not adopt; it is the only test that fails when the component is wrapped in `React.memo`.
  - **A controlled `activeIndex`** that differs from the active tab sets its state during render. Starting the transition's timer, or reporting an immediate change, happens in an effect, a commit later. The class did both in the lifecycle.
  - **A click still in its transition** loses to a controlled change. The class cleared its timer. Here the click finds out when it fires, through the counter ProgressSteps uses, so it drops itself even if it fires between that commit and the effect.
  - **Items that changed by value**, still compared with `isEqual`, refresh the cached tabs and contents and end a transition's faded state, without cancelling its click.
  - **The handle.** Function slots, content and children still receive one object for the component's lifetime, with `props`, `state`, `tabs`, `contents` and `setTab`, as `this` was. Nothing under UI Render reads it: the mapper's content and slots are bound `Render` calls, and `Render` ignores an object where its index goes.
  - **A cost, measured.** A parent render now runs the body twice, because the state update that records the new props re-renders at once, as the documented pattern does. So function content and slots are called twice per parent render, where the class called them once. Under UI Render each call only creates an element.

  **One behaviour changed, measured first and pinned.** A parent that followed a click by passing the tab back as `activeIndex` was told about it twice: `[1, 1]` under test on React 16, 17 and 18, with the items rebuilt as the mapper builds them. The class compared with the committed tab, which the timer's update had not reached when the parent's arrived in the same batch. Compared with the current state, the click is reported once. Under `<StrictMode>` the class also drew React's warning about `UNSAFE_componentWillReceiveProps`, and a test pins that there is none now.

  Two tests read the class instance through a ref: `@withTimer`'s `timers` array, and `this` as the argument the slots receive. They now use the timer spies and the handle's identity, and both pass against the previous class. Ten tests are new. Two fail against the previous class, on the double report and the StrictMode warning, and eight pass against it:
  - the last of two clicks wins;
  - a controlled change with `transitionUpdate` drops a pending click and waits its own 50 ms;
  - a controlled change applies at once, `transitionUpdate` or not, when the active tab is gone;
  - resetting an uncontrolled index is not reported;
  - a change of items during a click ends the faded state and keeps the click, controlled and uncontrolled;
  - a tab the parent did not adopt goes back on the parent's next render;
  - `setTab(index, false)` from function content switches at once and drops a pending click.

  Eighteen mutations each fail at least one test. In the demo, clicks on Tabs, Tabs Buttoned and Tab List, and the controlled hidden tabs of Dynamic Layout, behave as they did with the class, with the same console output: one prop-type warning that predates this change, because Dynamic Layout's items have no `tab`. It was the prop type that was too strict, and #114 made `tab` optional, as `meta.schema.json` already had it.

  **`TableView` converted 2026-09-29**, the eighth leaf. It is `React.memo` of a function, like the `PureComponent` it was: its lifecycle compared the new props with the previous ones, so a parent render with equal props had nothing to do.
  - **`sorts` that change by value** replace the sort state during render. Only the previous `sorts` are kept for that, not the props object, so a parent render runs the body once, as the class rendered once. Only a change of `sorts` runs it twice, for the state update during render; measured with a counting cell renderer.
  - **The class's two cached getters are two caches with the same keys.** The sorted rows are kept until the items change element by element (`isEqualList`) or the sort state changes. A test pins that the same elements in a new array keep their order, as they did. The headers derived from the first item are kept until an item with other keys arrives, and an empty list keeps them.
  - **What renderers receive.** Cell and header renderers were handed `this`, and the engine uses it: `transforms.js` reads `props.name` off it for nested paths, and looks handlers up on it by the name the meta gives, `onClick: 'handleItemExpand'` and `onChange: 'handleToggleExpandAll'`. They now get one object for the component's lifetime, with `props`, `state` and every member the class had, and each handler keeps one identity. Five tests reached the class instance: four through a ref, three of them through one shared helper, and one with `expect.any(TableView)`. They now read that object, and pass against the class as well.
  - **`handleSort`** still computes the new sort from the last render's state, as it read `this.state`, because it reports that sort before returning.

  **One behaviour changed, measured first and pinned.** The expansion handlers wrote a copy of `this.state` back. Inside one batch `this.state` does not move, so of two `handleItemExpand` calls only the last survived, and a `handleToggleExpandAll` batched before an expansion was lost: `["C"]` where `["A", "C"]` was asked for, on React 16, 17 and 18. They now update from the current state. The demo never showed the difference: its tables behave the same with either version, for sorting in "All Possible Configurations", row expansion and Expand All in "Table: Nested", and paging in "Table Pagination". Under `<StrictMode>` the class's `UNSAFE_componentWillReceiveProps` warning is gone too.

  Eight tests are new. Two fail against the previous class, on the batch and the StrictMode warning, and six pass against it:
  - a parent render with equal props does not render again;
  - renderers get one object with the current props and handlers, and a handler finds a row by key in the current items;
  - a clicked sort survives parent renders that pass equal `sorts`;
  - the sort cache is keyed by element;
  - derived headers survive an empty list;
  - ~~a page past the end, after the items shrink, renders no rows~~ (fixed 2026-10-05, below).

  That last one was **a candidate defect, pinned rather than fixed**: `activePage` was never brought back into range. Fourteen mutations each fail at least one test. **FIXED 2026-10-05.** A page past the end now moves back to the last page, corrected during render as the sort state is; `Pagination`, which clamps the page it shows, already marked that page as current. Measured in Chromium through the `/demo` sandbox, with 25 rows on page 3 and then 12 passed to the mounted table: master rendered no rows under a pagination showing page 2, and the fix renders R10 and R11. Two tests replace the pin, and both fail on the previous code. The second proves the state is corrected, not only the slice: rows that grow back after fitting on one page start from page 1, where the old code returned to page 3.

  **Found while measuring, and left for their own changes.** Every header without a sort passes `onClick={false}` to its `Row`, which React reports as a listener that is not a function: six warnings in "All Possible Configurations", with the class and the function alike. The same example also warns that `InputNumber` receives a boolean `error` where it expects a string.

  **`InputNative` converted 2026-09-29**, the ninth and last leaf, and the only one in TypeScript. It is `React.memo` of a function, like the `PureComponent` it was: its lifecycle compared `value` and `compact` with the previous props.
  - **The resize moved from before the render to after the commit.** The lifecycle resized a compact input during the render phase: to a changed value, or to what the input held when only `compact` changed. That now happens in a layout effect, which React runs after the DOM is updated and before the browser paints. The same effect takes over what render itself did to the DOM, where a controlled color input took a changed value as its background.
  - **On the server it is a plain effect,** chosen once, when the module loads, by whether there is a `window`. With a layout effect there, a server render warns "useLayoutEffect does nothing on the server" once for every input, on React 16, 17 and 18.3 alike (measured by a mutation that used it). A host that server-renders a form would get that for every field. A test renders on the server, in a Node environment, and asserts no console error.
  - **The ref callbacks keep one identity**, as the class's instance fields did. A new callback on every render would be called on every render, resizing again and calling `onMount` again.

  **Nothing else changed.** It was measured against the class on React 16, 17 and 18, and is the same in all of these:
  - the widths and backgrounds across value, offset and type changes;
  - one `onMount`;
  - no render for a parent render with equal props.

  The class's StrictMode warning about `UNSAFE_componentWillReceiveProps` is gone. In the demo, typing into the Input and Integer examples and toggling the checkboxes behave the same with either version, with the same console output. No example renders a compact or a color input.

  Eight lifecycle tests and the server test are new. Only the StrictMode test fails against the previous class; the others pass against it. Of nine mutations, eight each fail at least one test. The ninth, a plain effect in the browser as well, passes everything, because jsdom does not paint. The before-paint timing rests on React's contract for layout effects, not on a test.

  **That was the last leaf.** No component outside the engine has an `UNSAFE_*` lifecycle left, and the next item is the engine's design pass (the order above).

  **A trap for the next five `@withTimer` leaves: `jest.getTimerCount()` is not a count of a component's timers.** The first version of these tests used it, and it passed on React 18 but failed on the React 16 and 17 legs: 2 where 1 was expected. The older scheduler keeps a fake timer of its own, pending or not depending on which test ran first, so the other count assertions had passed only because of test order.
  **THE ENGINE: DESIGN PASS, 2026-09-29.** Measured before anything was decided, and nothing in `src` changed. There were two instruments:
  - a `Proxy` returned from the `UIRender` constructor, which recorded every read and write on an instance for the whole suite: 2816 tests, 534 instances in 29 files, each access attributed to the file that made it;
  - each of the six `UNSAFE_*` definitions swapped for its naive safe replacement, the suite run each time, and the change reverted.

  **What renders a document.** Four classes, three of which are one component at runtime:
  - **`WithForm`** (`modules/form/utils.js`) renders react-final-form's `<Form>`. It owns the form object, its subscription and the document's `formsStorage` entry, and its render prop renders the rest with `instance={this}`.
  - **The form layer (`withFormSetup`) over the engine layer (`UIRenderLifecycle`, `rules.js`) over the declared `UIRender`.** This combined class is `Active.UIRender`, and its instance is what the engine hands every node as `instance`.
  - **A nested document** (`engine/Data.js`) renders the combined class directly, sharing its parent's form through `parent`. With a form of its own (`useForm`) it renders the whole wrapper instead.
  - **`Render.js`** is the error boundary, and it stays a class whatever else happens: React has no hook for `componentDidCatch`.

  **The six `UNSAFE_*` definitions, and what each one does before a render:**

  | | where | what it does |
  |---|---|---|
  | 1 | `UIRender`, `componentWillReceiveProps` | copies changed `data`, `meta` and `meta.currencyCode` props into state |
  | 2 | engine layer, `componentWillMount` | builds `submit`, which merges the nested documents' values before submitting, and registers a nested document with its parent |
  | 3 | engine layer, `componentWillUpdate` | drops the cached `meta` when the state object changed |
  | 4 | engine layer, `componentWillReceiveProps` | copies changed `data` and `meta` into state again, through updaters, then calls 1 |
  | 5 | form layer, `componentWillReceiveProps` | recomputes `canSave` against the incoming props, by swapping them into `this._props`, and reprocesses the errors |
  | 6 | `WithForm`, `componentWillReceiveProps` | when `initialValues` changed by value, resets the form to them and re-registers the document in `formsStorage` |

  Numbers 1 and 4 write the same two keys. 1 runs second, and its plain `setState({data: {json}})` is the write that lands.

  **The instance is internal.** No host can reach it. The published entry is a function component (`declare function UIRender(props)` in the public types), so there is no ref to hold. Everything that reads the instance is in `src`, which makes its contract a design choice rather than a compatibility constraint.

  One undocumented channel exists, found in slice 1. The form layer calls `onChangeState(instance)` whenever `canSave` changes, and `onChangeState({})` when it unmounts. The prop is declared only in the form layer's propTypes and its code comment: it is in neither the public types nor `docs/`. Nothing in `src` passes it, but an untyped host could. The conversion keeps both calls, the first with the instance object.

  **What reads it, measured.** Outside the instance's own layers there are 14 members: 12 reached by the suite, and 2 more found by a static scan of `src` (`isUnmounting` and `formValues`). By member:
  - `translate`: the mapper, 9,296 reads.
  - `state`: `Render.js` and `transforms.js`, for `currencyCode`. Templates too: `{state.x}` resolves against the instance itself (`interpolateString(template, instance)`), so `state` is a free-form bag that meta's `setState` actions write by path and meta's templates read.
  - `props`: fields, the period validator, `dataKindPush`, templates, and the error report (`props.onError`).
  - `form`: `asField` and `asInputDateField`.
  - `formValues`: the period validator.
  - `setState`: `dataKindPush`, on the parent.
  - `getRawFormsData`: `showIf`.
  - `popupById` and `popupTemplates`: the mapper writes them while rendering a `Popup` node, and `popupOpen` reads them at click time.
  - `_autoSubmit` and `submit`: `autoSubmit.js` keeps its timer on the instance.
  - `dataKindPath` and `getDataKind`: the period validator.
  - `isUnmounting`: a field's removal path.

  A nested document reads its **parent's** `form`, `handleSubmit`, `onDataChanged`, `translate`, `state`, `setState` and the three registry methods, and the parent's `props.instance`, which is the parent's `WithForm`, for its form. `WithForm` itself offers `form` and `handleSubmit`, to the form layer through `props.instance`.

  **Three things write to the instance during render**, and a hooks form has to give each a home:
